COME UP WITH THE BEST IMPLEMENTATION FOR A TEMPORAL LEVEL HARNESS WHILE HAVING THE FLEXIBILITY AT ATOMIC LEVEL # Temporal-Level Durable Harness with Atomic Flexibility — Final Implementation ## 1. Architecture Boundaries (4 Layers + Gateway) ``` ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ Gateway / Zero-Trust Proxy (mTLS + WebTransport + Capability Auth)│ │ - Validates Ed25519/HMAC signatures per request │ │ - Issues capability tokens; maps cert SAN → agent/session identity │ │ - Routes to Agent Runtime (Worker / Sandbox / Process) │ └───────┬──────────────┬──────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┘ │ │ │ ▼ ▼ ▼ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌────────────────┐ │ Main Agent │ │ Sub-Agent │ │ Tool / MCP │ │ (Durable) │ │ (Durable) │ │ Server │ │ WebTransport │ │ WebTransport │ │ TLS/TCP │ └──────┬───────┘ └──────┬───────┘ └────────────────┘ │ │ └────────────────┘ │ ┌─────────────▼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ Execution Runtime (Workers, Sandbox.create, Deno.Command, QUIC) │ └─────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ Atomic invocation envelope (signed) ┌─────────────▼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ Atomic Registry (injectable primitives, sandbox profiles, policies) │ └─────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ Durable interfaces only ┌─────────────▼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ Harness Core (DAG compiler, replay engine, version markers, saga) │ └─────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ DurableState adapter ┌─────────────▼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ Durable State (Deno KV: events, results, timers, signals, locks) │ │ Separate Audit Store (append-only, HMAC-signed, out-of-context) │ └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ``` Boundary rules: - **Harness Core** never imports `Deno.Kv`, `fetch`, `crypto.subtle`, `Worker`, or `Sandbox`. It receives effects via `PrimitivePorts`. - **Atomic Registry** owns primitive resolution (`name@version`), schema validation, sandbox profiles, retry policies, and capability requirements. - **Durable State** is the only adapter that touches `Deno.Kv`. It implements `DurableTransaction` with `check/set/delete/enqueue`. - **Execution Runtime** executes primitives selected by the Core. It manages process replacement when agents die. --- ## 2. Durable State — KV Schema & Transaction Model Use array keys (not slash strings) to avoid ambiguity. ```typescript const k = { workflowMeta: (t,w) => ["workflows",t,w,"meta"], runMeta: (t,w,r) => ["workflows",t,w,"runs",r,"meta"], runHead: (t,w,r) => ["workflows",t,w,"runs",r,"head"], event: (t,w,r,s) => ["workflows",t,w,"runs",r,"events",s], signalHead: (t,w) => ["workflows",t,w,"signals","head"], signal: (t,w,s) => ["workflows",t,w,"signals",s], timer: (t,w,r,n) => ["workflows",t,w,"runs",r,"timers",n], actionResult: (ik) => ["actionResults",ik], task: (q,id) => ["tasks",q,"items",id], taskLease: (q,id) => ["tasks",q,"leases",id], session: (t,s) => ["sessions",t,s], sessionEvent: (t,s,seq) => ["sessions",t,s,"events",seq], refreshHash: (t,h) => ["sessions",t,"refreshTokens",h], raceLock: (t,n) => ["locks",t,n], audit: (t,at,id) => ["audit",t,at,id], }; ``` Event types (immutable append-only): `WorkflowStarted | VersionMarker | ActivityScheduled | ActivityCompleted | ActivityFailed | TimerScheduled | TimerFired | SignalReceived | ChildStarted | ChildCompleted | ChildFailed | CompensationScheduled | NodeCompleted | NodeFailed | ContinueAsNew | WorkflowCompleted | WorkflowFailed` All events are appended with `kv.atomic()` comparing `runHead` (`check` previous `versionstamp`, `set` event + new head). This guarantees exactly-once event ordering per run. Audit records are written to a separate `auditKv` (different file path or credential namespace) and signed with HMAC. Workflow code has no read access to audit keys. --- ## 3. Atomic Registry — Injection & Boundaries Every atomic action is registered independently of any workflow definition. ```typescript interface AtomicAction { (input: I, ctx: AtomicExecutionContext, deps: ActionDependencies): Promise; } interface ActionSpec { name: string; version: string; inputSchema: Json; outputSchema: Json; capabilities: CapabilityRequirement[]; sandbox: SandboxProfile; // kind: worker | sandbox | command | microvm timeoutMs: number; retry: RetryPolicy; requiresProviderIdempotency: boolean; execute: AtomicAction; idempotencyKey?: (input: Json) => string; } interface SandboxProfile { kind: "worker" | "microvm" | "command" | "in-process"; allowNet?: string[]; allowRead?: string[]; allowWrite?: string[]; env?: Record; memoryMb?: number; timeoutMs: number; region?: string; } class AtomicRegistry { #map = new Map(); // key = "name@version" register(spec: ActionSpec): void { /* validate schemas, sandbox, policies */ } resolve(name: string, version: string): ActionSpec; } ``` Execution uses an explicit envelope: ```typescript interface AtomicCall { tenantId: string; workflowId: string; runId: string; commandId: string; idempotencyKey: string; action: string; actionVersion: string; input: Json; inputHash: string; capabilityToken: string; issuedAt: number; expiresAt: number; nonce: string; traceParent?: string; signature: string; // HMAC-SHA256 or Ed25519 over canonical JSON } ``` Before execution: 1. `PermissionPort.verify()` validates capability token against `spec.capabilities`. 2. `DurableState.get(k.actionResult(idempotencyKey))` checks cached result. 3. If cached, return immediately (replay hit) and write audit `ATOMIC_REPLAY_HIT`. 4. If not cached, invoke `ExecutionPort.execute(profile, fn, timeoutMs)`. 5. After success/failure, write result via `kv.atomic()` comparing previous `versionstamp`; only one writer succeeds. 6. Write audit to separate store. The unsafe failure window (`external effect succeeds` → `crash` → `retry`) requires that either: - The external provider accepts `idempotencyKey` and guarantees idempotency; or - The workflow uses a durable outbox + compensation (Saga). No workflow logic accesses audit data. --- ## 4. Harness Core — Temporal Workflows as Deterministic DAG + Replay ### 4.1 Temporal Configuration & Workflow Definition ```typescript interface TemporalConfig { workflowIdReuse: "reject" | "allow-after-terminal"; continueAsNewAfterEvents?: number; continueAsNewAfterBytes?: number; defaultActivityTimeoutMs: number; defaultRetry: RetryPolicy; signalRetentionMs: number; timerSweepIntervalMs: number; } interface DagNode { id: string; kind: "action" | "child" | "timer" | "signal" | "join" | "race"; dependsOn?: string[]; action?: { name: string; version: string; input: Json; compensation?: CompensationSpec; retry?: RetryPolicy; }; child?: { workflow: string; version: string; input: Json; }; timer?: { delayMs?: number; at?: number; }; signal?: { name: string; timeoutMs?: number; }; retry?: RetryPolicy; fallback?: string[]; raceGroup?: string; resourceCost?: { cpuMs: number; memoryMb: number; networkRequests: number; }; } interface WorkflowDefinition { name: string; version: string; temporal: TemporalConfig; nodes: DagNode[]; limits: { maxNodes: number; maxDepth: number; maxParallelism: number; maxPayloadBytes: number; maxCpuMs: number; maxMemoryMb: number; }; execute?: (ctx: WorkflowContext, input: Json) => Promise; } ``` Validation rules (`validateDag`): - Cycle detection via DFS (`O(V+E)`). - Dependency references exist. - `resourceCost` sums within limits per execution path. - `maxDepth` enforced. - `graphHash` computed deterministically (canonical JSON of sorted nodes/edges). ### 4.2 Replay State Machine The harness supports two modes: **functional replay** (re-running `execute()` with a replay context) and **direct DAG execution**. For maximum temporal compatibility, the replay model uses `ReplayWorkflowContext` that throws `YieldWorkflow` to suspend execution at pending effects. ```typescript class ReplayCursor { constructor(private history: WorkflowEvent[], private results: Map) {} wasScheduled(commandId: string): boolean; version(changeId: string, min: number, max: number): number; activityResult(commandId: string, idempotencyKey: string): Json | undefined; } class ReplayWorkflowContext implements WorkflowContext { async activity(nodeId: string, action: string, version: string, input: Json, opts?: { retry?: RetryPolicy; compensation?: CompensationSpec }): Promise { const commandId = `${run.runId}:activity:${nodeId}`; const inputHash = await crypto.sha256(canonicalJson(input)); const idempotencyKey = await crypto.sha256(`${run.tenantId}|${run.workflowId}|${run.runId}|${commandId}|${action}@${version}|${inputHash}`); const cached = this.cursor.activityResult(commandId, idempotencyKey); if (cached !== undefined) return cached as T; if (!this.cursor.wasScheduled(commandId)) { await scheduleDurableTask({ ... }); } throw new YieldWorkflow(`activity:${commandId}`); } async sleep(nodeId: string, delayMs: number): Promise { const timerId = `${run.runId}:timer:${nodeId}`; if (history includes TimerFired for timerId) return; if (!history includes TimerScheduled) await scheduleTimer({ ... }); throw new YieldWorkflow(`timer:${timerId}`); } async waitForSignal(name: string): Promise { const signal = await findUnconsumedSignal(state, run.run, name); if (!signal) throw new YieldWorkflow(`signal:${name}`); return signal.payload as T; } async child(nodeId: string, workflow: string, version: string, input: Json): Promise { const childRun = await ensureChildStarted(run, nodeId, workflow, version, input); if (childRun.result !== undefined) return childRun.result as T; throw new YieldWorkflow(`child:${childRun.runId}`); } version(changeId: string, min: number, max: number): number { return this.cursor.version(changeId, min, max); } continueAsNew(summary: Json): never { throw new ContinueAsNewSignal(summary); } } ``` Replay algorithm for a run: 1. Load `runHead` → `nextSeq`. 2. Read all events `0..nextSeq-1` into `ReplayCursor`. 3. Load `actionResults` for all `idempotencyKey` values present in `ActivityScheduled` events into `results` map. 4. Execute `def.execute(ctx, run.input)`. 5. If `YieldWorkflow` thrown, suspend; the durable task/timer/signal/child mechanism is already responsible for wakeup. 6. If completed, append `WorkflowCompleted` event. 7. If `ContinueAsNewSignal`, execute atomic `continueAsNew()` (create new run ID, write `ContinueAsNew` event, update workflow meta). Replay equality test: ```typescript const first = await driveWorkflow(def, state, input); const replayed = await replayWorkflow(def, state, input); assert.deepStrictEqual(first.decisions, replayed.decisions); assert.strictEqual(fakeNetwork.requests.length, 1); // no duplicate side effects ``` ### 4.3 Determinism Constraints Banned inside workflow functions: - `Date.now()` (use `ctx.now()` backed by event timestamps or deterministic clock) - `Math.random()` (use deterministic PRNG seeded by `run.runId`) - Direct `fetch()` or `crypto.subtle` - Process environment access - Unordered object iteration without explicit sort The harness provides deterministic APIs: - `ctx.now()` → reads from replay cursor or event timestamp. - `ctx.random()` → seeded deterministic random. - `ctx.activity()` → only executes via DurableState + AtomicRegistry. --- ## 5. Temporal Features — Concrete Implementation ### 5.1 Signals Signal ingestion (separate from workflow execution): ```typescript async function deliverSignal(kv: Deno.Kv, tenantId: string, workflowId: string, signal: SignalRecord): Promise { const idKey = k.signalById(tenantId, workflowId, signal.signalId); const existing = await kv.get(idKey); if (existing.value) return; // duplicate delivery const headKey = k.signalHead(tenantId, workflowId); for (;;) { const head = await kv.get<{ nextSeq: number }>(headKey); const seq = head.value?.nextSeq ?? 0; const commit = await kv.atomic() .check(existing) .check(head) .set(idKey, signal) .set(k.signal(tenantId, workflowId, seq), signal) .set(headKey, { nextSeq: seq + 1 }) .enqueue({ kind: "signal", tenantId, workflowId, signalId: signal.signalId, signalSeq: seq }) .commit(); if (commit.ok) return; } } ``` `kv.watch()` observes `k.signalHead()` changes, but the consumer must `kv.list()` the full signal stream from its last consumed sequence (watch may coalesce mutations). ### 5.2 Timers Timer durability uses `kv.enqueue()` with `delay`. A `TimerScheduled` event, a `TimerRecord` in KV, and a queue message with `keysIfUndelivered` are committed together. ```typescript interface TimerRecord { timerId: string; run: RunRef; fireAt: number; status: "WAITING" | "FIRED" | "CANCELLED"; } async function scheduleTimer(run: RunRef, timerId: string, delayMs: number, state: DurableState): Promise { const fireAt = Date.now() + delayMs; await appendEventAndEnqueue(state, run, { type: "TimerScheduled", timerId, fireAt }, { taskId: `timer:${timerId}`, queue: "timers", kind: "timer", run, availableAt: fireAt }, { delay: delayMs }); } async function fireTimer(kv: Deno.Kv, state: DurableState, task: TaskEnvelope): Promise { const timerId = task.taskId.slice("timer:".length); const timerKey = k.timer(task.run.tenantId, task.run.workflowId, task.run.runId, timerId); const timer = await kv.get(timerKey); if (!timer.value || timer.value.status !== "WAITING") return; const next = { ...timer.value, status: "FIRED" as const }; const commit = await kv.atomic() .check(timer) .set(timerKey, next) .enqueue({ kind: "workflow-wakeup", run: task.run, reason: "timer", timerId }) .commit(); if (!commit.ok) return; await state.appendEvent(task.run, { type: "TimerFired", timerId }); } ``` A `Deno.cron()` job (`"* * * * *"`) scans overdue timer records (`fireAt < now`, status `WAITING`) and repairs missed queue messages. ### 5.3 Child Workflows Child creation writes both parent event (`ChildStarted`) and child run meta atomically: ```typescript interface ChildSpec { nodeId: string; workflowName: string; version: string; input: Json; parent: RunRef; } async function startChild(state: DurableState, parentRun: RunRef, spec: ChildSpec): Promise { const childWorkflowId = `${parentRun.workflowId}/${parentRun.runId}/child/${spec.nodeId}`; const childRunId = await crypto.sha256(`${childWorkflowId}|${spec.version}`); const childMeta: RunMeta = { run: { tenantId: parentRun.tenantId, workflowId: childWorkflowId, runId: childRunId }, status: "RUNNING", definitionVersion: spec.version, graphHash: await resolveGraphHash(spec.workflowName, spec.version), input: spec.input, parentRunId: parentRun.runId, createdAt: Date.now(), updatedAt: Date.now(), }; await state.commit(tx => tx .set(k.runMeta(...childMeta.run), childMeta) .set(k.child(..., childMeta.run), { parentRunId: parentRun.runId, nodeId: spec.nodeId }) .set(k.event(..., 0), { type: "ChildStarted", childRunId, workflowId: childWorkflowId }) .enqueue({ kind: "child-start", ...childMeta.run })); return childRunId; } ``` The parent waits via `ReplayWorkflowContext.child()` which checks for `ChildCompleted` event or throws `YieldWorkflow`. The child runs independently and writes `ChildCompleted` to its own event history; a watch or queue listener copies it to the parent. ### 5.4 Saga & Compensation Each `DagNode` may declare `action.compensation`. The harness maintains a durable `compensationStack` per run (stored in KV or derived from event history). On workflow failure: 1. Stop scheduling new nodes. 2. Read completed nodes in reverse order from event history. 3. For each completed node with compensation, schedule `CompensationScheduled` event and durable task. 4. Execute compensation activities via Atomic Registry (with separate idempotency keys). 5. Append `CompensationCompleted` or `CompensationFailed`. 6. Mark workflow `FAILED` or `COMPENSATED` based on compensation results. Compensations must be independently retryable and must not reference failed agent memory. ### 5.5 Race Conditions (Durable Locks) ```typescript interface LockRecord { owner: string; acquiredAt: number; expiresAt: number; winnerResultRef?: string; } async function tryAcquireRace(kv: Deno.Kv, tenantId: string, lockName: string, owner: string, now: number): Promise { const key = k.raceLock(tenantId, lockName); const current = await kv.get(key); if (current.value && current.value.expiresAt > now && current.value.owner !== owner) return false; const record: LockRecord = { owner, acquiredAt: now, expiresAt: now + 30000 }; const commit = await kv.atomic().check(current).set(key, record).commit(); return commit.ok; } ``` Exactly one competitor wins via CAS. Losers either wait (polling `kv.get()` with backoff) or execute configured fallback nodes. The winner writes its result to `LockRecord.winnerResultRef` for others to retrieve. ### 5.6 Continue-As-New Limits history growth. Triggered by `ctx.continueAsNew()` or automatically when `continueAsNewAfterEvents` / `continueAsNewAfterBytes` exceeded. ```typescript interface ContinueAsNewSpec { oldRun: RunRef; summary: Json; // minimal state required to continue } async function executeContinueAsNew(state: DurableState, ports: PrimitivePorts, spec: ContinueAsNewSpec): Promise { const newRunId = await crypto.sha256(`${spec.oldRun.runId}|continue-as-new|${ports.clock.now()}`); const newRun: RunRef = { ...spec.oldRun, runId: newRunId }; await state.commit(tx => tx .set(k.runMeta(spec.oldRun.tenantId, spec.oldRun.workflowId, spec.oldRun.runId), { ...currentMeta, status: "CONTINUED", continueAsNewFrom: spec.oldRun.runId, }) .set(k.runMeta(...newRun), { run: newRun, status: "RUNNING", input: spec.summary, parentRunId: spec.oldRun.runId, createdAt: ports.clock.now(), }) .set(k.event(spec.oldRun.tenantId, spec.oldRun.workflowId, spec.oldRun.runId, nextSeq), { type: "ContinueAsNew", nextRunId: newRunId, summaryHash: await crypto.sha256(canonicalJson(spec.summary)), }) .enqueue({ kind: "run-start", ...newRun }) ); return newRun; } ``` The new run starts fresh with `runHead` reset; the old run remains auditable. --- ## 6. Multi-Agent Runtime & Resumable Sessions Agents are replaceable executors, not workflow owners. Workflow truth lives in KV. ### 6.1 Agent Spawning & Replacement ```typescript async function spawnAgent(spec: AgentSpec, ports: PrimitivePorts): Promise { const cmd = new Deno.Command(Deno.execPath(), { args: ["run", "--allow-net=127.0.0.1", "--deny-read", "--deny-write", "agent.ts"], env: { AGENT_ID: spec.agentId, AGENT_PORT: String(spec.port), ACTION_SET: spec.actionSet.join(",") }, stdout: "piped", stderr: "piped", }); return { agentId: spec.agentId, pid: cmd.spawn().pid, close: () => cmd.kill("SIGTERM") }; } async function replaceAgent(sessionId: string, newAgentId: string, state: DurableState): Promise { const session = await state.get(k.session(...)); await state.set(k.session(...), { ...session.value, agentId: newAgentId, status: "ACTIVE" }); // Replay session events + current workflow signals to new agent } ``` Agents expose WebTransport endpoints (`Deno.upgradeWebTransport`) with mTLS (`Deno.listenTls`). The Gateway validates certificates and extracts SAN → agent identity. ### 6.2 Resumable Session Flow Sessions are independent of workflow runs but reference them. ```typescript interface SessionMeta { sessionId: string; tenantId: string; subject: string; status: "ACTIVE" | "REVOKED" | "EXPIRED"; currentAgentId?: string; lastEventSeq: number; currentRunId?: string; createdAt: number; expiresAt: number; } interface RefreshTokenRecord { hash: string; // SHA-256 of refresh token sessionId: string; rotatedAt: number; } ``` Resumption: 1. Client sends refresh token. 2. Gateway hashes token (`crypto.subtle.digest("SHA-256")`), looks up `RefreshTokenRecord`. 3. Validates session active, rotation state, revocation. 4. Atomically deletes old hash and writes new hash for rotation. 5. Issues short-lived signed access token (`capabilityToken`) for current agent/workflow. 6. Restores session: reads session events from `lastEventSeq`, replays signal history to agent, passes current `AtomicCall` envelopes for any pending workflow tasks. If the agent process died, the Gateway starts a new agent (Worker or Sandbox) and sends reconstructed session projection plus current workflow state (not full context window). ### 6.3 Zero-Trust Security Integration Per Atomic Call Every atomic call carries its own authorization context; no global session object is used inside workflow execution. Flow: 1. Client authenticates (Passkey or signed request) → Gateway verifies. 2. Gateway creates `capabilityToken` (signed HMAC/Ed25519) with scopes (`resource`, `operations`, `constraints`). 3. Gateway creates `AtomicCall` envelope with `idempotencyKey`, `commandId`, `capabilityToken`, `signature`. 4. Proxy validates mTLS client cert (`Deno.connectTls` with CA verification) and maps SAN to allowed agent/session. 5. Harness verifies `capabilityToken` against `spec.capabilities`. 6. Runtime executes in selected isolation (`Worker`, `Sandbox.create`, `Deno.Command`). 7. After execution, result stored by `idempotencyKey`; audit record signed and appended to separate store. 8. Telemetry span (`OpenTelemetry`) includes `tenant_id`, `workflow_id`, `run_id`, `command_id`, `action`, `idempotency_key`. --- ## 7. Observability & Telemetry Instrument at the `DurableState` adapter level and inside `AtomicRegistry` invocation wrapper. ```typescript interface TelemetryPort { span(name: string, attrs: Record, fn: () => Promise): Promise; event(name: string, attrs: Record): void; gauge(name: string, value: number): void; } ``` Dashboard consumes: - `DurableState` event streams (via `kv.watch()` on run heads or custom projection keys). - Audit records (via `auditKv.list()`). - Metrics pushed via UDP or WebTransport stream (CPU, GPU, token usage, retry counts, lease expirations). Every workflow node produces a span tree: `Harness → ReplayLoop → WorkflowExecution → DAGBatch → ActivityExecution → AtomicCall → Sandbox/Worker → NetworkRequest` --- ## 8. Implementation Phases | Phase | Deliverable | |-------|-------------| | 0 | `DurableState` adapter over `Deno.openKv()`; `AuditStore` separate file; core interfaces (`PrimitivePorts`, `DurableTransaction`). | | 1 | `AtomicRegistry` with schema validation, sandbox profiles; register 10+ primitives (`crypto.hash`, `net.request`, `memory.get`, `schedule.enqueue`). | | 2 | `ReplayCursor`, `ReplayWorkflowContext`, replay equality tests; `YieldWorkflow` mechanism. | | 3 | `HarnessCore`: DAG compiler, `validateDag`, `executeWorkflow` (direct mode first, replay mode second). | | 4 | Temporal primitives: `SignalHandler` (delivery + watch), `TimerManager` (enqueue + fire + cron repair), `ChildWorkflowManager` (start + complete propagation). | | 5 | Saga/compensation: durable compensation stack, reverse execution, audit logging. | | 6 | Zero-trust: `ZeroTrustSecurity` (capability tokens, HMAC signing, audit isolation), session/refresh rotation, `replaceAgent`. | | 7 | Gateway: WebTransport server (`QuicEndpoint`, `upgradeWebTransport`), mTLS validation, agent spawning/replacement, proxy routing. | | 8 | Observability: OpenTelemetry spans, dashboard consuming KV projections, metrics endpoint. | | 9 | Hardening: race conditions (`LockRecord` CAS), circuit breakers in runtime, resource limits per sandbox, deterministic replay testing. | --- ## 9. Key Constraints & Design Decisions - **No mutable global state.** Every layer receives interfaces explicitly. - **Every external effect has an idempotency key.** Replay skips it. - **Every event is append-only.** No updates, only new versions via `kv.atomic()`. - **Audit is physically/logically separate.** Workflow replay never reads audit. - **Sessions and workflows are decoupled.** Agent death does not corrupt workflow state. - **Sandbox isolation is configurable.** `Worker` for CPU-bound trusted code; `@deno/sandbox` (`Sandbox.create`) for untrusted custom actions; `Deno.Command` with restricted flags for CLI tools. - **WebTransport (QUIC) is the agent-agent protocol.** TCP/TLS is for external ingress and database connections; UDP is only for telemetry. - **Replay equality is tested.** Every workflow change requires a replay regression test confirming identical `decisions` and zero duplicate side effects. This architecture achieves Temporal-level durable execution (resumable, replayable, signal-aware, child-workflow-capable, saga-protected) with atomic-level flexibility (pluggable primitives, sandbox profiles, capability-bound authorization, replaceable storage/network/crypto providers) built entirely on native Deno APIs. ## Elaborate Plan for Agent Orchestration on Deno This document provides a comprehensive architecture and implementation plan for a distributed agent orchestration system built on Deno. The system features a proxy/gateway, multiple agent nodes (main and sub-agents) on isolated subdomains/ports, toolkits (MCP, file, network), zero‑trust security with mutual TLS, resumable sessions, fine‑grained permissions, secure storage, and full observability. The architecture is layered to expose clean interfaces, enable open‑source contribution, and support deterministic workflow graphs with fallback and resilience strategies. --- ### 1. High‑Level Architecture Overview ``` ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ User / External Client │ └─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┘ │ HTTPS / WSS ┌─────────────────────────▼───────────────────────────────────┐ │ Proxy / API Gateway │ │ - TLS termination, authentication, routing │ │ - Permission enforcement (zero‑trust) │ │ - Session & state management │ │ - Request/response logging & telemetry │ │ - Internal communication: mTLS, WebTransport, QUIC │ └──────┬────────────┬────────────┬─────────────┬────────────┘ │ │ │ │ ┌───▼───┐ ┌───▼───┐ ┌───▼───┐ ┌───▼───┐ │ Main │ │ Sub │ │ Sub │ │ File │ │Agent │ │Agent │ │Agent │ │Server │ │Port A │ │Port B │ │Port C │ │Port F │ └───────┘ └───────┘ └───────┘ └───────┘ │ │ │ │ └────────────┴────────────┴─────────────┘ │ ┌─────────▼─────────┐ │ Deno KV / Queue │ │ + File System │ │ (encrypted) │ └───────────────────┘ ``` - **Proxy/Gateway**: The single entry point for all external and internal requests. It handles authentication, authorization, routing, and orchestrates agent lifecycles. - **Agents**: Each agent runs as an independent Deno process, listening on its own port/subdomain, exposing a secure RPC interface. Agents can spawn sub‑agents. - **Tool Servers**: Dedicated servers for specific capabilities (file access, network, MCP) with their own permission policies. - **Storage**: Deno KV for ephemeral state (sessions, permissions, queues, watch); encrypted files for long‑term logs, artifacts, and key material. - **Observability**: OpenTelemetry + custom dashboard with real‑time metrics, traces, and per‑agent detail views. --- ### 2. Communication Protocols – When to Use Which | Protocol | Use Case | |------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------| | **UDP** | High‑frequency, loss‑tolerant telemetry (e.g., heartbeat, metrics push). Not for control. | | **TCP (plain)** | Internal low‑trust diagnostics or debugging only; never for production (no security). | | **TLS/TCP** | **Default** for all secure, reliable, bidirectional communication between proxy, agents, and tool servers. Use `Deno.listenTls` and `Deno.connectTls` with mutual authentication (client certificates). | | **WebSockets** | Persistent full‑duplex channels for browser clients and for agent‑to‑proxy real‑time event streaming when low latency and message framing are important. | | **WebTransport** | Modern alternative to WebSockets over HTTP/3 (QUIC). Use when you need multiplexed streams, low‑latency, and unordered delivery for certain RPCs or media. | | **QUIC** | As underlying transport for WebTransport; can also be used directly for custom protocols needing fast connection establishment and mobility. | **Recommendation**: Use TLS/TCP for all inter‑service communication (agents, proxy, tools). Use WebSockets for browser‑agent interaction. Consider WebTransport for high‑performance, low‑latency agent‑to‑agent streaming if needed. --- ### 3. Zero‑Trust Security Architecture - **Identity**: Each agent, tool server, and the proxy has a unique **public/private key pair** (Ed25519). Certificates (X.509) are issued by an internal Certificate Authority (CA) and rotated periodically. - **Mutual TLS**: Every connection requires client certificate verification; the proxy validates the certificate and maps the subject to an agent identity. - **Authentication**: User authentication uses a passkey (WebAuthn) or public‑key signature. Users sign requests with their private key; proxy verifies signatures. - **Permissions** are stored in Deno KV, keyed by (user, agent, resource). They have scopes: - `allowOnce`, `denyOnce` – single use. - `allowSession`, `denySession` – for the current session. - `allowAlways`, `denyAlways` – persistent. - `allowUser`, `denyUser` – for a user session. - **Session Resumability**: Sessions are stored in KV with TTL; each session has a unique ID and a refresh token. The proxy can resume a session by validating the refresh token. - **Encryption at Rest**: All stored files (logs, artifacts) are encrypted using AES‑GCM with keys derived from a master key (managed outside, e.g., via environment variable or HSMs). Use `Deno.ensureFile`, `Deno.writeFile` with encryption. --- ### 4. Storage and Logging - **Deno KV** (`Deno.openKv`): - Sessions, permissions, agent registrations, queue items, watched keys. - Use atomic transactions for consistency (e.g., updating permission state). - Watch for changes to trigger workflows (e.g., new task enqueued). - **File System**: - Encrypted logs: structured JSON lines (`std/log`) written to rotating files. - Artifacts (tool outputs, uploaded files) stored in encrypted directories. - Agent checkpoints for resumable sessions. - **Hashing**: - Use SHA‑256 for integrity checks, deduplication (content‑addressable storage), and password hashing (with bcrypt/Argon2 via `node:crypto` or Web Crypto). - Use HMAC for message authentication. - **Logging Strategy**: - All requests, permissions decisions, agent state changes are logged to files with a correlation ID. - Logs are **not** appended to the agent’s context to avoid bloat; instead, use a logging service that can be queried via the dashboard. - **Context Summarization**: Periodically compress agent context (conversation history) using a summarization model (e.g., via LLM) and store in KV. --- ### 5. Orchestration – Scheduling, Queues, and Workers - **Deno KV + Watch**: Use `kv.watch()` to listen for changes on keys (e.g., new tasks in a queue) and trigger handlers. This is the backbone for reactive orchestration. - **Deno Queues**: Built on KV, they provide reliable, at‑least‑once delivery with retries and delay. Use for asynchronous work like sending emails, processing large files, or scheduling agent actions. - **Deno Cron**: For periodic housekeeping (e.g., cleanup expired sessions, rotation of logs, health checks). - **Scheduler**: Implement a custom `Scheduler` that enqueues delayed tasks using a queue with a `delay` property, or use `setTimeout` with persistence. - **Workers**: - **Worker Threads** (`new Worker`) – for CPU‑intensive tasks (e.g., encryption, image processing) or to isolate sandboxed code. Each worker runs in a separate thread. - **Worker Pool** – manage a pool of workers to limit concurrency and reuse them for many tasks. Use a shared queue to distribute work. - **Sandboxing**: - Use Deno’s **Sandbox API** (`Deno.createSandbox`) to run untrusted code (e.g., user‑submitted agents) with resource limits, memory caps, and timeouts. - For terminals and browsers, run them inside a sandboxed environment with restricted network and file access. --- ### 6. Observability and Telemetry - **OpenTelemetry**: Instrument all components with traces and metrics. Export to a collector (e.g., OTLP) or directly to a backend (Prometheus, Grafana). - **Metrics**: CPU, memory, GPU (if applicable), network I/O, request latencies, agent state counts. - **Dashboard**: - Built as a Deno web app (e.g., using Fresh or React) that consumes metrics via REST/WebSocket. - **Agent State View**: Show each agent’s status, current task, input/output, and a code‑level console stream (agent thoughts, planning steps, tool calls). - **Timeline View**: Similar to Temporal, visualising workflow execution with start/end times, dependencies, and sub‑workflows. - Use `Deno.perf` and `Deno.memoryUsage` for real‑time system metrics. --- ### 7. Layered Function Design (Atomic → Workflows → DAG) The codebase is organized in layers, each exposing a clear interface. #### Layer 0: Core Utilities - `crypto.ts`: key generation, signing, verification, encryption, hashing. - `log.ts`: structured logging with levels, rotation, correlation IDs. - `kv.ts`: helpers for Deno KV operations (get, set, atomic, watch). - `network.ts`: TLS server/client setup, WebSocket helpers, WebTransport helpers. - `permissions.ts`: permission data structures and evaluation engine. #### Layer 1: Atomic Functions (Low‑level primitives) These are standalone, testable functions. | Function | Description | |----------|-------------| | `createAgent(config)` | Creates an agent identity (key pair, port, subdomain) and stores in KV. | | `registerAgent(agentId, publicKey, endpoint)` | Registers agent with proxy. | | `startListener(type, port, handler, options)` | Starts a TCP/TLS/WebSocket/WebTransport listener. | | `connectTo(endpoint, options)` | Establishes a secure connection to another service. | | `sendRequest(conn, request)` | Sends a structured RPC request over the connection. | | `listenForResponse(conn, timeout)` | Waits for a response with timeout. | | `storePermission(subject, resource, action, scope, value)` | Saves a permission rule in KV. | | `checkPermission(subject, resource, action)` | Evaluates permissions (with caching). | | `encryptFile(path, data, key)` | Encrypts and writes data to file. | | `decryptFile(path, key)` | Reads and decrypts file. | | `hash(data)` | Returns SHA‑256 digest. | | `generateUUID()` | Creates a v4 UUID. | #### Layer 2: Work Actions (Composed Primitives) These combine atomic functions into higher‑level operations. | Function | Description | |----------|-------------| | `createAgentTool(config)` | Creates a tool (e.g., file server) with its own permissions and registers it. | | `startWaitAndOffloadToSchedule(action, delay)` | Schedules an action to run after a delay using Deno Queue. | | `startWaitWithListenThenPoll(port, handler, pollInterval)` | Starts a listener and polls for incoming requests; returns a promise that resolves when a response is received. | | `ProxyVerifyTCPIncoming(conn)` | Verifies the client certificate and identity of an incoming TCP/TLS connection. | | `ProxySpawnSubAgent(parentAgentId, config)` | Spawns a new sub‑agent process with given configuration and passes the parent’s public key. | | `ProxySpawnMainAgent(config)` | Spawns the main agent with full configuration and permissions. | #### Layer 3: Workflows (Orchestrated Actions) Workflows are defined as sequences of work actions with error handling. | Workflow | Description | |----------|-------------| | `DelegateWebSearchToSubAgentAndCheckBack(searchQuery, parentCtx)` | Spawns a sub‑agent that performs web search; monitors its listener; upon response, validates and continues. | | `DelegateTaskToSubAgentAndPlanForward(task, parentCtx)` | Delegate a complex task to a sub‑agent; while listening, update the plan for next steps. | | `ProcessFileUpload(file, userPermissions)` | Encrypts file, stores it, and logs the action. | | `HandlePermissionRequest(user, resource, action)` | Presents a permission UI and stores the decision. | | `ResumeSession(sessionId, refreshToken)` | Validates refresh token and restores session state from KV. | #### Layer 4: DAG and Strategies (Top‑Level Abstraction) Define workflows as **directed acyclic graphs (DAGs)** with nodes being tasks (work actions) and edges denoting dependencies. - **Constraints**: Each task has a max duration, retry policy, and fallback. - **Heuristic Check**: Before instantiation, the DAG is verified for cycles and resource bounds. - **Configuration**: - `allowDelegations`: boolean - `availableTools`: list - `maxTime`: total workflow duration - `maxContextSize`: token limit for summarization - `planningCapability`: which LLM to use for planning - `maxConcurrency`: number of parallel tasks - `cacheStrategy`: e.g., race‑to‑fill cache or use cached results. The engine executes the DAG, respects timeouts, and implements fallback branches when a task fails. --- ### 8. Detailed Function Specifications (Selected) Below are key function signatures with brief descriptions. ```typescript // crypto.ts export function generateKeyPair(): { publicKey: Uint8Array; privateKey: Uint8Array }; export function sign(privateKey: Uint8Array, data: Uint8Array): Uint8Array; export function verify(publicKey: Uint8Array, signature: Uint8Array, data: Uint8Array): boolean; export function encryptAES(key: Uint8Array, plaintext: Uint8Array): { ciphertext: Uint8Array; iv: Uint8Array }; export function decryptAES(key: Uint8Array, iv: Uint8Array, ciphertext: Uint8Array): Uint8Array; // kv.ts export async function kvGet(key: Deno.KvKey): Promise; export async function kvSet(key: Deno.KvKey, value: T, ttl?: number): Promise; export async function kvAtomic(op: Deno.AtomicOperation): Promise; export async function kvWatch(keys: Deno.KvKey[], callback: (changes: T[]) => void): Promise; // network.ts export async function startTlsListener(port: number, cert: string, key: string, handler: (conn: Deno.Conn) => void): Promise; export async function connectTls(host: string, port: number, caCert: string): Promise; export function upgradeToWebSocket(request: Request): Promise; export function createWebTransportServer(port: number): Promise; // permissions.ts export type PermissionScope = 'allowOnce' | 'denyOnce' | 'allowSession' | 'denySession' | 'allowAlways' | 'denyAlways' | 'allowUser' | 'denyUser'; export function storePermission(subject: string, resource: string, action: string, scope: PermissionScope, value: boolean): Promise; export function checkPermission(subject: string, resource: string, action: string): Promise; // agent.ts export async function createAgent(config: AgentConfig): Promise; export async function spawnSubAgent(parentId: string, config: SubAgentConfig): Promise; export async function agentListen(agent: Agent, port: number): Promise; export async function agentSendRequest(agentId: string, target: string, request: any): Promise; // workflow.ts export type WorkflowNode = { id: string; action: WorkAction; dependsOn?: string[]; fallback?: string; timeout?: number }; export type WorkflowDefinition = { nodes: WorkflowNode[]; config: WorkflowConfig }; export function validateDAG(definition: WorkflowDefinition): boolean; export async function executeWorkflow(definition: WorkflowDefinition, input: any): Promise; // scheduler.ts export function scheduleDelayed(action: () => Promise, delayMs: number): Promise; export function startCronJob(schedule: string, handler: () => Promise): void; ``` --- ### 9. Important Workflows and Pipelines (Examples) #### 9.1. Web Search Delegation Pipeline 1. **User** sends a request to proxy: "Search for X". 2. Proxy authenticates user, checks permission to spawn search agent. 3. Proxy creates a **main agent** (if not already) with search capability. 4. Main agent spawns a **sub‑agent** on a new port, passing the search query. 5. Sub‑agent uses a tool (e.g., external search API) and listens for responses. 6. While waiting, main agent continues planning with `DelegateTaskToSubAgentAndPlanForward`. 7. Sub‑agent returns results; main agent validates and sends back to user. #### 9.2. Permission Request Flow 1. Agent attempts to access a file; proxy checks permission → not found. 2. Proxy triggers `HandlePermissionRequest` which sends a prompt to user (via UI). 3. User selects one of the scopes (allow once, allow session, etc.). 4. Proxy stores the decision in KV and notifies the agent. 5. Agent proceeds or fails accordingly. #### 9.3. Resumable Session Workflow 1. User connects, authenticates with passkey → proxy creates session (stored in KV). 2. Session includes a refresh token; client receives it. 3. If connection drops, client reconnects with refresh token. 4. Proxy calls `ResumeSession`, restores agent context, and continues. #### 9.4. DAG‑based Research Workflow - Nodes: `[FetchSources, Summarize, Analyze, GenerateReport]`. - Dependencies: Summarize depends on FetchSources; Analyze depends on Summarize; GenerateReport depends on Analyze. - Fallback: If FetchSources fails, use cached sources. - Max time: 5 minutes total. - Engine executes with concurrency 1 (sequential) or can parallelize if no dependencies. --- ### 10. Deno Libraries and Features Utilised | Feature / Library | Purpose | |-------------------|---------| | `Deno.serve`, `Deno.listenTls` | HTTP & TLS servers | | `Deno.connectTls` | TLS client connections | | `Deno.serve` with `upgradeWebSocket` | WebSocket servers | | `Deno.serve` with `WebTransport` | WebTransport support | | `Deno.Kv` | Persistent key‑value store with atomic ops, watch | | `Deno.Queue` | Reliable message queue | | `Deno.Cron` | Scheduled jobs | | `Deno.Worker` | Multi‑threading | | `Deno.Sandbox` | Secure microVMs for untrusted code | | `std/log` | Structured logging | | `std/collections` | Data manipulation | | `std/crypto` | Additional crypto helpers | | `std/testing` | BDD testing | | `std/encoding` | Base64, hex, etc. | | `node:crypto` | For compatibility with npm packages | | `@opentelemetry/api` | OpenTelemetry instrumentation | | `@std/async` | Debounce, delay, etc. | | `jsr:@deno/gfm` | Markdown rendering for dashboard | --- ### 11. Security and Resilience - **Rate Limiting**: Use token bucket or sliding window (implement via KV). Apply to all endpoints. - **Exponential Backoff**: For retries (e.g., tool calls), use `@std/async/retry` with custom backoff. - **Graceful Failure**: On error, agent logs the failure, cleans up child processes, and releases ports. Use `try/finally` and `AbortController`. - **Cleanup**: Use `Deno.addSignalListener` to catch SIGTERM/SIGINT and perform orderly shutdown. - **Resource Limits**: Each agent has a memory limit (via `Deno.memoryUsage` monitoring) and timeouts. --- ### 12. Implementation Roadmap 1. **Phase 0**: Set up project structure, core utilities (crypto, logging, KV helpers). 2. **Phase 1**: Implement atomic functions (Layer 1) with comprehensive tests. 3. **Phase 2**: Build work actions (Layer 2) and integrate with Deno’s networking. 4. **Phase 3**: Create workflow engine (Layer 3 & 4) with DAG validation and execution. 5. **Phase 4**: Develop the proxy/gateway and agent spawning logic. 6. **Phase 5**: Implement permissions, sessions, and zero‑trust features. 7. **Phase 6**: Add observability (OpenTelemetry, dashboard). 8. **Phase 7**: Sandboxing and worker pools. 9. **Phase 8**: Hardening, scaling, and performance tuning. --- ### 13. Conclusion This architecture leverages Deno’s built‑in capabilities (KV, queues, cron, workers, sandbox, TLS, WebTransport) to build a secure, extensible agent orchestration system. The layered design ensures that low‑level primitives can be reused, workflows are deterministic and resilient, and the system provides full observability. The zero‑trust model with fine‑grained permissions and resumable sessions makes it suitable for enterprise‑grade applications. By following this plan, the implementation will be robust, maintainable, and open‑source ready. The extensive use of Deno’s standard libraries reduces external dependencies and keeps the codebase lean. Building a secure, highly concurrent, zero-trust multi-agent system is one of the most complex architectural challenges right now. Because you are using Deno, you have a massive advantage: web-standard cryptography, built-in key-value storage (`Deno KV`), WebTransport, and granular permission flags (`--allow-net`, `--deny-read`) are all native to the runtime. Here is the comprehensive architectural blueprint to orchestrate this system securely and deterministically. ## 1. Network Topology: Gateway vs. Proxy You should build an **API Gateway with an Embedded Reverse Proxy**, not just a simple proxy. A standard reverse proxy (like Nginx) routes traffic blindly. In a zero-trust multi-agent network, you need a Gateway written in Deno that dynamically evaluates state: checking if an agent's session is revoked in `Deno KV`, verifying WebAuthn passkey signatures, and translating external WebSockets into internal WebTransport streams. ### When to use which protocol: | Protocol | Primary Use Case in Your System | Why? | | --- | --- | --- | | **WebTransport (over QUIC)** | **Agent-to-Agent / Agent-to-Proxy** | The ultimate inter-agent protocol. It solves "head-of-line blocking." A sub-agent can stream a 50MB file to the proxy on one stream, while simultaneously sending JSON-RPC task updates on another, over the same connection. | | **TCP / TLS** | **External Ingress & DBs** | The backbone. Use `Deno.serve({ cert, key })` for the initial Gateway entry, standard REST toolkits, and connecting to Postgres/SQLite. | | **UDP** | **Telemetry & Observability** | High-frequency, loss-tolerant data. Use `Deno.listenDatagram` for agents blasting CPU/RAM/Network metrics to your monitoring dashboard where dropping a packet doesn't matter. | | **WebSockets** | **Browser-to-Gateway** | Full-duplex streams for legacy web frontends and streaming the real-time observability console (thought loops, logs) to the user's UI. | --- ## 2. Zero-Trust Security & State Do not store logs, sessions, or permissions in the LLM's context window—that will cause immediate context collapse and token bloat. ### Authentication & Permissions Storage 1. **The User Keyring (Native App):** Wrap your Deno Gateway in a lightweight Tauri/Rust app. This acts as an enclave. It stores the user's root private key securely in the OS Keyring and handles native OS file-picker dialogues. 2. **Session Resumption & Tokens:** When the user signs a request with a Passkey (`WebAuthn`), the Gateway issues a cryptographically signed capability token (like a Macaroon or JWT). 3. **Where to Store State:** Store active session leases, granular permissions (`allow_once`, `deny_agent_session`), and request logs in **Deno KV**. ### Cryptography Routing * **Encryption (`crypto.subtle` AES-256-GCM):** Use this to encrypt agent memory dumps or files written to the local disk. Never write raw plaintext agent outputs if the system is multi-tenant. * **Hashing (SHA-256):** Use hashing for deduplication. Hash an agent's context or tool inputs to check if a cached response already exists. * **Hashing (HMAC-SHA256):** Use this to sign internal requests between the Gateway and Sub-Agents to ensure an external process isn't spoofing an internal port. --- ## 3. Orchestration: KV, Schedulers, and Concurrency Your orchestration engine is a Deterministic Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG). Before execution, the system must validate the graph for cycles to prevent deadlocks. The heuristic check runs in $O(V + E)$ time to ensure no sub-agent is waiting on a parent agent that is waiting on the same sub-agent. ### State Mechanisms * **Deno KV `watch`:** This replaces complex pub/sub brokers (like Redis). When the Main Agent spawns a task, it writes to `["task", "sub-agent-1", "pending"]`. The sub-agent uses `kv.watch()` to instantly react when that key changes. * **Atomic Transactions (`kv.atomic()`):** Use this for locks. If two agents try to access the same file router, `kv.atomic().check().commit()` ensures only one succeeds. * **Deno Queues (`kv.enqueue`):** Use for resilient tool execution. Queues natively handle rate limiting, exponential backoff, and dead-lettering if a network tool fails. * **Deno Cron:** Use strictly for background cleanup—purging dead sessions, rotating logs, and sweeping orphaned agent data. ### Execution Models | Model | When to use | | --- | --- | | **Worker Threads** | For CPU-bound tasks within an agent (e.g., parsing a massive PDF, calculating local embeddings). | | **Worker Pool** | To manage the LLM harness. You should pool your agents so you don't exceed your system's max concurrency and crash the host machine. | | **Sandboxes / `Deno.Command**` | Use `Deno.Command` with strict flags (`--deny-net`, `--deny-write`) to execute Python/JS code written by the agent. If on Deno Deploy, use Subhosting MicroVMs. | --- ## 4. Layered Architecture & Function Specifications Here is the exact codebase structure and function abstraction you need to build this. ### Layer 1: Core Gateway & Security Primitives These handle zero-trust validation before any LLM logic runs. * `ProxyVerifyTCPIncoming(req, cert, pubKey)`: Intercepts all traffic. Verifies the HMAC signature of the requesting agent or the Passkey of the user. Returns an execution context or drops the connection. * `ProxySpawnMainAgent(config, permissions)`: Provisions a dynamic port, assigns a session ID, writes the permission bounds to Deno KV, and boots the Worker pool. * `ProxySpawnSubAgent(parentKey, scope, tools)`: Spawns an agent on a new local subdomain, granting it a subset of the parent's permissions (Principle of Least Privilege). ### Layer 2: Async Orchestration & Resilience These handle the realities of network failure and timing. * `startWaitAndOffloadToSchedule(task, delay)`: Pushes a task to a `Deno Queue` to be executed at a later time, freeing up the agent's thread. * `startWaitWithListenThenPoll(taskId)`: Uses `Deno KV watch` to suspend the agent's processing until a specific KV key updates (e.g., waiting for user approval on an `allow_once` file operation). * `startWaitWithListenThenRequestResilience(tool, maxRetries)`: Wraps tool execution in a circuit breaker. Implements jittered exponential backoff if the external API rate-limits the agent. ### Layer 3: Workflows & Pipelines (The Agent Harness) These functions define standard agent behaviors. * `DelegateWebSearchToSubAgentAndCheckBack(query, context)`: 1. Pauses the parent agent. 2. Spawns a search sub-agent via WebTransport. 3. The sub-agent calls `CallToolContinue()`, evaluates results, and streams data back. 4. Parent agent resumes upon receiving the `StreamEnd` signal. * `DelegateTaskToSubAgentAndPlanForward(task)`: Asynchronous fire-and-forget. The parent agent delegates the task, pipes the listener into a stack, and immediately moves to the next item in its planning phase. ### Layer 4: The DAG Compiler & Orchestrator The top-level controller. * `CompileAndValidateDAG(workflowNodes, constraints)`: Takes the configuration (`max_time`, `max_context`, `tools_available`, `cache_checking_allowed`) and compiles it. If the graph contains a deadlock loop or exceeds max concurrency limits, it fails gracefully before spawning a single process. * `EmitTelemetrySpan(agentId, metrics)`: Pushes CPU/GPU, token usage, and thought-loop logs via UDP to the observability dashboard. > **Key insight:** By decoupling the orchestration state (Deno KV) from the transport layer (WebTransport) and the execution boundary (Workers/Sandboxes), you ensure that if one agent hallucinates, crashes, or gets stuck in an infinite loop, the Gateway can cleanly terminate its Worker and revoke its KV session without taking down the main app. This is exactly the direction I think agent frameworks need to evolve. Not "an agent that calls tools." Rather, an **operating system for cognition**, where everything is deterministic, composable, observable, resumable and cryptographically verifiable. Reading your notes, I'd actually split the system into **12 abstraction layers**, each of which only exposes interfaces upward. The implementation remains replaceable. --- # Overall Architecture ``` User │ Passkey Authentication │ ────────────────────────────────────────────── Gateway / Zero Trust Proxy ────────────────────────────────────────────── Session Manager Permission Manager Certificate Manager Audit Logger Router Scheduler Event Bus ────────────────────────────────────────────── Agent Runtime Layer ────────────────────────────────────────────── Workflow Engine ────────────────────────────────────────────── Planning Engine ────────────────────────────────────────────── Memory Layer ────────────────────────────────────────────── Tool Runtime ────────────────────────────────────────────── MCP Servers ────────────────────────────────────────────── Browser Terminal Filesystem Web Databases ────────────────────────────────────────────── Deno Runtime ────────────────────────────────────────────── ``` Every layer only knows the layer below it. Never upward. --- # Layer 0 The Runtime Pure Deno. Use * KV * Queues * Cron * Workers * Sandbox * OpenTelemetry * WebCrypto * WebGPU * WebTransport * HTTP3 * TLS * WebSockets No business logic. --- # Layer 1 Communication This is surprisingly the most important layer. Every communication primitive lives here. Example ``` SendHTTPRequest() SendWebSocket() SendWebTransport() SendUDP() SendTCP() SendTLS() ListenTCP() ListenUDP() ListenWebSocket() ListenWebTransport() OpenQUICConnection() CloseConnection() Reconnect() Ping() Heartbeat() KeepAlive() GracefulDisconnect() ``` No agent logic. Only communication. --- # When to use each protocol ## HTTP Configuration Discovery Authentication Tool invocation Downloads REST One-shot requests --- ## WebSocket Agent streaming Continuous logs Live console Token streaming Dashboard updates Chat NOT for unreliable telemetry. --- ## WebTransport Perfect for agents. Use for bidirectional streams partial reliability parallel streams interruptible transfers video browser control large context movement tool execution live collaboration Eventually this becomes your primary protocol. --- ## QUIC Never expose directly. WebTransport runs over QUIC. QUIC endpoint only serves WebTransport HTTP3 --- ## UDP Very few places. Use only for heartbeat service discovery LAN discovery broadcast metrics fire-and-forget telemetry NOT agent communication. --- ## TCP/TLS Agent-to-agent authentication Certificate exchange secure RPC proxy filesystem router MCP communication Anything requiring guaranteed delivery. --- # Layer 2 Security Everything here is Zero Trust. Every request includes ``` Agent ID Certificate Public key Signature Nonce Timestamp Session ID ``` Proxy verifies ``` VerifySignature() VerifyCertificate() VerifyPermission() VerifyNonce() VerifySession() VerifyExpiration() VerifyRevocation() ``` Never trust localhost. Treat localhost exactly like internet. --- # Layer 3 Identity Objects ``` Agent User Proxy Tool MCP Server Worker Sandbox ``` Each gets ``` UUID Keypair Certificate Capabilities Permissions Metadata ``` Functions ``` CreateKeypair() RotateKeys() IssueCertificate() RevokeCertificate() ExportPublicKey() ImportPublicKey() ``` --- # Layer 4 Permissions Every permission becomes ``` Capability Token ``` Example ``` Read Files Write Files Spawn Agent Open Browser Internet Network GPU Terminal Clipboard Memory Microphone Camera ``` Permission functions ``` RequestPermission() GrantPermission() DenyPermission() StoreDecision() ExpireDecision() RevokePermission() ``` Decision storage ``` Allow Once Allow Session Allow Agent Session Allow Always Deny Once Deny Session Deny Always ``` Exactly as you described. --- # Layer 5 Storage Never mix storage. Split it. ## Deno KV Session state Agent state Workflow state Locks Queues Small metadata Permission cache Heartbeat --- SQLite Logs Memory Context Conversations Indexes Embeddings metadata --- Blob storage Files Images Contexts Snapshots Large prompts Artifacts --- Vector DB Semantic memory Retrieval Embeddings --- # Layer 6 Scheduler Functions ``` Schedule() Cancel() Pause() Resume() Delay() Retry() Backoff() Timeout() Heartbeat() Watch() AwaitSignal() Poll() ``` Internally KV Queue Cron Worker --- Use Queue when background execution --- Use Cron when time based --- Use KV Watch when event driven --- # Layer 7 Workers Worker Thread CPU intensive Parsing Embeddings Compression Encryption Hashing Planning Graph compilation --- Worker Pool Multiple independent jobs 100 browser tasks 100 embeddings 100 searches Many tool invocations --- Subprocess Terminal Git Python Node CLI Sandbox --- Sandbox Never trust Generated code Python Terminal npm External code --- # Layer 8 Agent Runtime Functions ``` CreateAgent() DestroyAgent() PauseAgent() ResumeAgent() SleepAgent() WakeAgent() RestartAgent() SnapshotAgent() CloneAgent() ForkAgent() MergeAgent() KillAgent() ``` --- Subagent ``` SpawnSubAgent() VerifySpawn() AssignTask() CancelTask() WaitForResult() JoinResult() DisposeSubAgent() ``` --- # Layer 9 Tool Runtime Every tool ``` Registered Signed Sandboxed Permissioned Observable Versioned ``` Functions ``` RegisterTool() CallTool() CancelTool() RetryTool() VerifyOutput() StreamOutput() CacheOutput() ``` --- # Layer 10 Workflow Engine This is where your idea becomes unique. Everything becomes DAG. Example ``` User ↓ Plan ↓ Spawn Search Agent ↓ Search ↓ Verify ↓ Summarize ↓ Return ``` Every node ``` Timeout Retries Fallback Expected output Validator Metrics ``` Compile ↓ Graph ↓ Static Analysis ↓ Cycle Detection ↓ Deadlock Detection ↓ Memory Check ↓ Resource Check ↓ Permission Check ↓ Only then execute. --- # Workflow primitives ``` Sequence Parallel Race Loop Retry Branch Merge FanOut FanIn Wait Join Reduce Scatter Barrier Mutex Semaphore ``` --- # Higher abstractions Exactly what you described. Example ``` DelegateSearchWorkflow() DelegateCodingWorkflow() DelegatePlanningWorkflow() DelegateBrowserWorkflow() DelegateResearchWorkflow() DelegateFilesystemWorkflow() ``` Each internally calls ``` Spawn Authenticate Schedule Listen Wait Validate Summarize Merge Dispose ``` --- # Planning Layer Planner never executes. Planner only builds DAG. Functions ``` Think() Estimate() Forecast() BreakTask() ScorePlan() EvaluateRisk() GenerateFallback() PredictFailure() EstimateCost() ``` --- # Execution Layer Never think. Only execute. ``` ExecuteNode() ExecuteGraph() ExecuteBranch() ExecuteParallel() ExecuteRetry() ``` --- # Memory Layer Split memory. Working Session Long Term Semantic Episodic Tool Cache Conversation Scratchpad --- Context should never grow. Pipeline ``` Raw Context ↓ Summarizer ↓ Compression ↓ Memory ↓ Retrieval ↓ Fresh Context ``` No append-only prompts. --- # Logging Everything. Everything. Agent Workflow Permission CPU GPU Memory Tool Latency Retries Errors Context Planning Execution Tokens Costs --- Store separately. Never in context. --- Telemetry OpenTelemetry Each node creates spans. ``` Agent ↓ Workflow ↓ Tool ↓ Browser ↓ Filesystem ↓ LLM ``` Timeline becomes automatic. --- Dashboard Almost Temporal. Views Agent Tree Workflow DAG Timeline Logs CPU GPU Memory Queues KV Workers Network WebSockets WebTransport Token Usage Context Retries Permissions Certificates --- Console Every node ``` stdout stderr thinking planning reasoning summary tool logs network memory ``` --- # Hashing Use hashes for Artifact IDs Context IDs Cache Keys Prompt IDs Workflow IDs Permission snapshots Configuration fingerprints Binary verification Audit chains Use digital signatures—not hashes alone—for identity and authorization. --- # Resilience Every node ``` Timeout ↓ Retry ↓ Exponential Backoff ↓ Circuit Breaker ↓ Fallback ↓ Escalation ↓ Human ``` --- # Atomic primitive functions I would aim for roughly 250-400 primitives grouped into small interfaces rather than a handful of large functions. Some representative groups are: * **Identity:** `CreateAgent`, `IssueCertificate`, `RotateKeys`, `VerifySignature` * **Networking:** `ConnectTLS`, `OpenWebTransportSession`, `ListenWebSocket`, `SendFrame` * **Scheduling:** `EnqueueTask`, `ScheduleAt`, `DelayUntil`, `CancelSchedule` * **Synchronization:** `AcquireLease`, `ReleaseLease`, `WaitForSignal`, `PublishEvent` * **Persistence:** `KVGet`, `KVSet`, `KVAtomicCommit`, `AppendAuditRecord` * **Workflow:** `CreateNode`, `LinkNodes`, `ValidateDAG`, `CompileWorkflow`, `ExecuteNode` * **Planning:** `GeneratePlan`, `EstimateResources`, `SelectStrategy`, `GenerateFallbackPlan` * **Memory:** `SummarizeContext`, `StoreMemory`, `RetrieveMemory`, `EvictWorkingSet` * **Security:** `AuthorizeCapability`, `VerifyNonce`, `CreateSession`, `ResumeSession` * **Observability:** `StartSpan`, `RecordMetric`, `StreamLogs`, `CaptureSnapshot` Each primitive should do **one thing only**, making higher-level workflows deterministic, testable, and reusable. ## The architectural principle The biggest shift I'd make compared to today's agent frameworks is to stop treating "agents" as the primary abstraction. Instead: ``` Cryptographic Identity ↓ Capabilities ↓ Deterministic Workflows (DAG) ↓ Execution Runtime ↓ Agents (stateless executors) ↓ Tools ``` In this model, agents become **replaceable workers**, while the workflow graph, permissions, identity, and state become the durable system. That makes pausing, resuming, migrating, replaying, auditing, and even replacing an LLM implementation straightforward without changing the orchestration itself. This is much closer to designing a distributed operating system than building another agent framework, and it naturally accommodates Deno's strengths: Web APIs, WebCrypto, KV, Queues, Cron, OpenTelemetry, Sandboxes, Workers, WebTransport, and HTTP/3. 0. Deno API constraints that affect the design Two Deno details are important: Deno KV queues are at-least-once, not exactly-once. A handler must use a durable task/result record and idempotency key. kv.enqueue() supports delayed delivery, retry backoff, and keysIfUndelivered; it can also be included in kv.atomic() transactions. Deno KV queue documentation(opens in new tab) kv.watch() is a wake-up mechanism, not an event-log reader. It may coalesce multiple mutations and only delivers the latest state. The executor must reread ordered events from KV after receiving a watch notification. Deno KV watch documentation(opens in new tab) The current Sandbox API is Sandbox.create() from @deno/sandbox; it is not Deno.createSandbox(). Deno Sandbox documentation(opens in new tab) WebTransport server support is currently unstable and uses Deno.QuicEndpoint, listener.accept(), and Deno.upgradeWebTransport(). Deno WebTransport example(opens in new tab) Deno.cron() is a recurring scheduler. In the CLI, its execution state is process-local; on Deploy, Deno schedules the registered jobs. It should be a reconciliation mechanism, not the primary storage for individual workflow timers. Deno Cron documentation(opens in new tab) The durable semantics below therefore come from KV records, CAS transactions, immutable events, and idempotent task execution—not from queue delivery, process lifetime, watch(), or Deno.cron() alone. 1. Architecture ┌────────────────────────────┐ │ Gateway / Proxy │ │ mTLS, tenant auth, routing │ └──────────────┬─────────────┘ │ WebTransport / HTTP3 ┌──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┐ │ │ │ ┌────────▼────────┐ ┌────────▼────────┐ ┌────────▼────────┐ │ Main Agent │ │ Sub-Agent │ │ Ephemeral Agent │ │ process │ │ process │ │ Sandbox/Worker │ └──────────────────┘ └─────────────────┘ └─────────────────┘ │ ┌───────────────────────────────────────▼────────────────────────────────────┐ │ Execution Runtime │ │ task leasing, process supervision, WebTransport, Worker/Sandbox execution │ └───────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┘ │ ┌───────────────────────────────────────▼────────────────────────────────────┐ │ Atomic Registry │ │ action metadata, capability requirements, provider resolution, invocation │ └───────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┘ │ ┌───────────────────────────────────────▼────────────────────────────────────┐ │ Harness Core │ │ deterministic workflow/DAG state machine, replay, retries, timers, signals │ │ child workflows, saga, fan-out/fan-in, races, continue-as-new │ └───────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┘ │ DurableState interface ┌───────────────────────────────────────▼────────────────────────────────────┐ │ Durable State │ │ Deno KV workflow state, events, results, tasks, timers, sessions, signals │ └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ┌────────────────────────────┐ │ Separate Audit Store │ │ audit KV / append-only file │ └────────────────────────────┘ 1.1 Boundary rules Harness Core Pure orchestration. It must not directly import: Deno.openKv() fetch crypto.subtle Date.now() Math.random() Deno.Command Worker Sandbox process globals agent connection state It receives all effects through interfaces. Its responsibilities are: validate and hash workflow definitions; construct deterministic command IDs; execute the workflow state machine; execute DAG scheduling decisions; replay event history; decide retry, fallback, compensation, and continue-as-new; handle workflow-level signals and timers; generate child-workflow commands; enforce deterministic version markers. Atomic Registry The registry is not an agent class and not a workflow implementation. It contains independently registered atomic operations and their policies: input/output schemas; action implementation; provider dependencies; required capabilities; sandbox profile; timeout; retry policy; idempotency requirements; resource limits; version. It is the only layer allowed to resolve an action name to executable code. Durable State An adapter around Deno KV or another implementation. It owns: KV reads and writes; optimistic CAS; immutable event append; task creation and leases; timer records; signal records; action results; session records; watch subscriptions. The Core sees DurableState, not Deno.Kv. Execution Runtime The runtime performs effects selected by the Core: task polling and leasing; action invocation; CPU-bound Worker execution; isolated command execution; @deno/sandbox execution; process spawning and replacement; WebTransport communication; mTLS gateway integration; OpenTelemetry spans; retry wakeups. The runtime does not own workflow truth. If an agent disappears, the runtime loses only its lease; the workflow remains in KV. 2. Core interfaces type Json = | null | boolean | number | string | Json[] | { [key: string]: Json }; type KvKey = readonly (string | number | bigint | Uint8Array)[]; interface Entry { key: KvKey; value: T | null; versionstamp: string | null; } interface DurableState { get(key: KvKey): Promise>; list(prefix: KvKey): AsyncIterable>; /** * Executes a KV CAS transaction. The callback must build an operation * from values read before the callback. */ commit( operation: (tx: DurableTransaction) => DurableTransaction, ): Promise<{ ok: true; versionstamp: string } | { ok: false }>; appendEvent( run: RunRef, event: WorkflowEvent, ): Promise<{ ok: true; seq: number } | { ok: false }>; appendAudit(record: AuditRecord): Promise; watch(keys: KvKey[]): AsyncIterable[]>; } interface DurableTransaction { check(entry: Entry): DurableTransaction; set(key: KvKey, value: unknown): DurableTransaction; delete(key: KvKey): DurableTransaction; enqueue( message: unknown, options?: { delay?: number; backoffSchedule?: number[]; keysIfUndelivered?: KvKey[]; }, ): DurableTransaction; } interface RunRef { tenantId: string; workflowId: string; runId: string; } interface PrimitivePorts { crypto: CryptoPort; signer: SignerPort; clock: ClockPort; network: NetworkPort; scheduler: SchedulerPort; execution: ExecutionPort; memory: MemoryPort; permissions: PermissionPort; telemetry: TelemetryPort; } interface ClockPort { now(): number; } interface CryptoPort { randomBytes(length: number): Promise; sha256(value: Uint8Array | string): Promise; canonicalJson(value: unknown): string; } interface SignerPort { sign(input: Uint8Array): Promise; verify(input: Uint8Array, signature: string, keyId: string): Promise; } interface NetworkPort { request( request: { url: string; method: string; headers: Record; body?: Uint8Array; }, policy: NetworkPolicy, ): Promise<{ status: number; headers: Record; body: Uint8Array; }>; } interface SchedulerPort { enqueue(task: TaskEnvelope, delayMs?: number): Promise; cancel(timerId: string): Promise; } interface ExecutionPort { execute( profile: SandboxProfile, fn: () => Promise, timeoutMs: number, ): Promise; spawnAgent(spec: AgentSpec): Promise; replaceAgent(agentId: string, spec: AgentSpec): Promise; } interface MemoryPort { get(key: string): Promise; put(key: string, value: T): Promise; } interface PermissionPort { verify( capabilityToken: string, requirement: CapabilityRequirement, call: AtomicCall, ): Promise; } interface TelemetryPort { span(name: string, attributes: Record, fn: () => Promise): Promise; } PrimitivePorts are constructed once for a process, but are passed explicitly. No workflow or atomic action obtains them from module-level mutable state. 3. KV key model The following are logical paths. In Deno KV they should be represented as arrays, not slash-concatenated strings. const k = { workflow: (tenant: string, workflowId: string) => ["workflows", tenant, workflowId] as const, run: (tenant: string, workflowId: string, runId: string) => ["workflows", tenant, workflowId, "runs", runId] as const, runMeta: (tenant: string, workflowId: string, runId: string) => ["workflows", tenant, workflowId, "runs", runId, "meta"] as const, runHead: (tenant: string, workflowId: string, runId: string) => ["workflows", tenant, workflowId, "runs", runId, "head"] as const, event: (tenant: string, workflowId: string, runId: string, seq: number) => ["workflows", tenant, workflowId, "runs", runId, "events", seq] as const, signalHead: (tenant: string, workflowId: string) => ["workflows", tenant, workflowId, "signals", "head"] as const, signal: (tenant: string, workflowId: string, seq: number) => ["workflows", tenant, workflowId, "signals", seq] as const, signalById: (tenant: string, workflowId: string, signalId: string) => ["workflows", tenant, workflowId, "signalsById", signalId] as const, task: (queue: string, taskId: string) => ["tasks", queue, "items", taskId] as const, taskLease: (queue: string, taskId: string) => ["tasks", queue, "leases", taskId] as const, actionResult: (idempotencyKey: string) => ["actionResults", idempotencyKey] as const, timer: (tenant: string, workflowId: string, runId: string, timerId: string) => ["workflows", tenant, workflowId, "runs", runId, "timers", timerId] as const, child: (tenant: string, parentRunId: string, childId: string) => ["children", tenant, parentRunId, childId] as const, session: (tenant: string, sessionId: string) => ["sessions", tenant, sessionId] as const, sessionEvent: (tenant: string, sessionId: string, seq: number) => ["sessions", tenant, sessionId, "events", seq] as const, refreshToken: (tenant: string, tokenHash: string) => ["sessions", tenant, "refreshTokens", tokenHash] as const, raceLock: (tenant: string, lockName: string) => ["locks", tenant, lockName] as const, }; The audit store must use a different KV database handle or a separate append-only storage namespace inaccessible to workflow code: const auditKv = await Deno.openKv("./audit.kv"); const workflowKv = await Deno.openKv("./workflow.kv"); For a hosted deployment, these should preferably be separate databases or separate storage credentials, not merely a prefix in the same database. 3.1 Workflow metadata interface WorkflowMeta { tenantId: string; workflowId: string; status: "RUNNING" | "COMPLETED" | "FAILED" | "CANCELLED"; currentRunId: string; definitionName: string; definitionVersion: string; graphHash: string; createdAt: number; updatedAt: number; } interface RunMeta { run: RunRef; status: "RUNNING" | "WAITING" | "COMPLETED" | "FAILED" | "CONTINUED"; definitionVersion: string; graphHash: string; input: Json; parentRunId?: string; continueAsNewFrom?: string; createdAt: number; updatedAt: number; } interface RunHead { nextSeq: number; stateHash: string; } 3.2 Events type WorkflowEvent = | { type: "WorkflowStarted"; run: RunRef; input: Json; definitionVersion: string; graphHash: string; } | { type: "VersionMarker"; changeId: string; version: number; } | { type: "ActivityScheduled"; commandId: string; action: string; actionVersion: string; idempotencyKey: string; inputHash: string; } | { type: "ActivityCompleted"; commandId: string; idempotencyKey: string; resultHash: string; } | { type: "ActivityFailed"; commandId: string; attempt: number; retryable: boolean; errorCode: string; } | { type: "TimerScheduled"; timerId: string; fireAt: number; } | { type: "TimerFired"; timerId: string; } | { type: "SignalReceived"; signalId: string; name: string; payloadHash: string; } | { type: "ChildStarted"; childWorkflowId: string; childRunId: string; } | { type: "ChildCompleted"; childRunId: string; resultHash: string; } | { type: "CompensationScheduled"; compensationId: string; action: string; } | { type: "NodeCompleted"; nodeId: string; resultHash?: string; } | { type: "NodeFailed"; nodeId: string; errorCode: string; } | { type: "ContinueAsNew"; nextRunId: string; summaryHash: string; } | { type: "WorkflowCompleted"; resultHash?: string; } | { type: "WorkflowFailed"; errorCode: string; }; Large inputs/results should be stored in an injected blob provider. Events contain hashes and references rather than unbounded payloads. 4. Atomic registry 4.1 Registration types interface CapabilityRequirement { resource: string; // e.g. "net:api.example.com" operations: string[]; // e.g. ["GET"] constraints?: Record; } interface RetryPolicy { maxAttempts: number; backoffMs: number; maxBackoffMs: number; retryableErrors: string[]; deterministicJitter?: boolean; } interface SandboxProfile { kind: "in-process" | "worker" | "command" | "microvm"; allowNet?: string[]; allowRead?: string[]; allowWrite?: string[]; env?: Record; memoryMb?: number; cpuMs?: number; timeoutMs: number; secrets?: string[]; } interface ActionDependencies { crypto: CryptoPort; network: NetworkPort; memory: MemoryPort; telemetry: TelemetryPort; } interface AtomicAction { ( input: I, context: AtomicExecutionContext, deps: ActionDependencies, ): Promise; } interface ActionSpec { name: string; version: string; inputSchema: Json; outputSchema: Json; capabilities: CapabilityRequirement[]; sandbox: SandboxProfile; timeoutMs: number; retry: RetryPolicy; /** * If true, the provider must accept an idempotency key and guarantee * duplicate calls have the same externally observable effect. */ requiresProviderIdempotency: boolean; execute: AtomicAction; } interface AtomicExecutionContext { tenantId: string; workflowId: string; runId: string; commandId: string; idempotencyKey: string; attempt: number; capabilityToken: string; traceParent?: string; } class AtomicRegistry { #actions = new Map(); register(spec: ActionSpec): void { validateSchema(spec.inputSchema); validateSchema(spec.outputSchema); validateSandbox(spec.sandbox); validateRetry(spec.retry); const key = `${spec.name}@${spec.version}`; if (this.#actions.has(key)) { throw new Error(`Action already registered: ${key}`); } this.#actions.set(key, spec); } resolve(name: string, version: string): ActionSpec { const spec = this.#actions.get(`${name}@${version}`); if (!spec) throw new Error(`Unknown atomic action ${name}@${version}`); return spec; } } Example custom registration: registry.register({ name: "http.getJson", version: "1.0.0", inputSchema: { type: "object", required: ["url"], properties: { url: { type: "string" } }, }, outputSchema: { type: "object", required: ["status", "body"], }, capabilities: [{ resource: "net:api.example.com", operations: ["GET"], }], sandbox: { kind: "microvm", allowNet: ["api.example.com:443"], memoryMb: 768, timeoutMs: 15_000, }, timeoutMs: 15_000, retry: { maxAttempts: 4, backoffMs: 500, maxBackoffMs: 30_000, retryableErrors: ["TIMEOUT", "HTTP_429", "HTTP_503"], }, requiresProviderIdempotency: false, async execute(input, ctx, deps) { const result = await deps.network.request( { url: input.url as string, method: "GET", headers: { "x-idempotency-key": ctx.idempotencyKey, }, }, { allowHosts: ["api.example.com"], }, ); return { status: result.status, body: JSON.parse(new TextDecoder().decode(result.body)), }; }, }); The registry must reject an action whose requested sandbox allows more resources than its capability requirements. For example, an action requiring net:api.example.com cannot receive an unrestricted allowNet: ["*"] profile. 5. Atomic invocation and zero-trust boundary Every call is authenticated and authorized independently. 5.1 Signed call envelope interface AtomicCall { tenantId: string; workflowId: string; runId: string; action: string; actionVersion: string; commandId: string; idempotencyKey: string; input: Json; inputHash: string; capabilityToken: string; issuedAt: number; expiresAt: number; nonce: string; traceParent?: string; signature: string; } The signature covers a canonical representation of all fields except signature: function unsignedCall(call: Omit): Uint8Array { return new TextEncoder().encode(canonicalJson(call)); } The signer may be: HMAC-SHA-256 for a single trusted tenant boundary; Ed25519 for distributed agents and asymmetric verification; a KMS/HSM-backed signing adapter; a test signer using deterministic test keys. WebCrypto implements Ed25519/HMAC where supported. The workflow does not call WebCrypto directly. 5.2 Invocation wrapper async function invokeAtomic( call: AtomicCall, registry: AtomicRegistry, state: DurableState, ports: PrimitivePorts, ): Promise { const spec = registry.resolve(call.action, call.actionVersion); await ports.permissions.verify( call.capabilityToken, { resource: `action:${spec.name}`, operations: ["execute"], constraints: { tenantId: call.tenantId, workflowId: call.workflowId, }, }, call, ); const existing = await state.get( k.actionResult(call.idempotencyKey), ); if (existing.value !== null) { await writeAudit(state, { type: "ATOMIC_REPLAY_HIT", tenantId: call.tenantId, workflowId: call.workflowId, runId: call.runId, action: call.action, commandId: call.commandId, idempotencyKey: call.idempotencyKey, resultHash: await ports.crypto.sha256( ports.crypto.canonicalJson(existing.value), ), at: ports.clock.now(), }); return existing.value; } const startedAt = ports.clock.now(); try { const output = await ports.telemetry.span( `atomic.${spec.name}`, { tenant_id: call.tenantId, workflow_id: call.workflowId, run_id: call.runId, command_id: call.commandId, action: spec.name, }, () => ports.execution.execute( spec.sandbox, () => spec.execute( call.input, { tenantId: call.tenantId, workflowId: call.workflowId, runId: call.runId, commandId: call.commandId, idempotencyKey: call.idempotencyKey, attempt: 1, capabilityToken: call.capabilityToken, traceParent: call.traceParent, }, { crypto: ports.crypto, network: ports.network, memory: ports.memory, telemetry: ports.telemetry, }, ), spec.timeoutMs, ), ) as Json; // This is a conditional write.
